0: looked at other window when heard that thing fall.
1: A light stand was lying on the floor when I looked.

5: Then you did her in with a single blow! Thwap!

Evidence list: we need find contradiction. Look at evidence descriptions.

Evidence 9: May Testimony: "The victim dodged an attack then ran to the right, but she was caught and struck."

That contradicts White's testimony that Mia ran to the left (testimony 2) and then turned and ran for the door (maybe right?). Also May says she ran to the right, not left. So evidence 9 (May Testimony) contradicts testimony 2 (White says run left). Also evidence 9 says she was caught and struck, but White says "you did her in with a single blow! Thwap!" That's consistent (single blow). But direction differs.

Thus pair: evidence 9, testimony 2.

Check spatial rule: "If XXX is in the same room as YYY then XXX can potentially see YYY" not relevant. "If XXX is at location YYY at time ZZZ then XXX cannot be at a different location at time ZZZ" could apply: At the moment of running, Mia cannot be both left and right. So rule: If at same time cannot be at different location. So we can cite rule: "If XXX is at location YYY at time ZZZ then XXX cannot be at a different location at time ZZZ". This reveals contradiction: May says Mia ran right, White says left.

Thus answer JSON: {"evidence": 9, "testimony": 2}

**Step 1:** The crime photo (Evidence 2) shows the umbrella “lies open in a distance” from the victim, while Phoenix’s testimony (Testimony 3) states that the victim “fell down on top of his cheap umbrella,” implying the umbrella was directly beneath the victim at the moment of the fall.

**Step 2:** The spatial reasoning rule that can be applied is:  
*If XXX is far from YYY then XXX cannot be directly under YYY* (derived from the general notion that “far” precludes immediate contact). Although not listed verbatim, the rule “If XXX is covered by YYY and YYY is far from ZZZ then XXX is far from ZZZ” demonstrates that distance contradicts direct contact.

**Step 3:** Applying this rule, the umbrella being “far” from the victim (as shown in the photo) contradicts the claim that the victim fell onto the umbrella (which would require the umbrella to be directly under the victim, i.e., not far).

**Step 4:** Even without a direct rule, the spatial contradiction is clear: an object cannot simultaneously be both “far away” and “directly beneath” another object at the same moment.

**Step 5:** The contradictory pair is:

{"evidence": 2, "testimony": 3}

Testimony 3: "A true gentleman fights only with his own fists... But they were not enough." Not spatial.

Testimony 4: "His first blow struck true! Bam! ...And that's all she wrote." Not spatial.

Now evidence: many. Need find spatial contradiction: e.g., evidence says Sacred Urn was in Atmey's office, but testimony says he was in a room with a wall etc. Or evidence about camera data: camera went off at 1 AM. Testimony says he looked away from door for a brief moment. Not contradictory.

Maybe evidence 6: Shichishito: "DeMasque knocked Atmey out during the crime w/ a blow to the back of the head." That says blow to back of head. Testimony 5 says blow landed upon my third eye (forehead). Contradiction: back of head vs forehead. That's evidence vs testimony. Evidence 6 says blow to back of head. Testimony 5 says blow to third eye (forehead). That's spatial contradiction.

Check rules: "If XXX has their back to YYY then XXX cannot see YYY" not relevant. But we can use rule: If XXX is in same room as YYY then can see. Not needed.

Now produce answer with analysis steps then final line.

**Step 1:** The testimonies of Luke Atmey describe the thief’s attack. Testimony 5 says: *“I put my back to the wall … but the thief's blow landed upon my third eye.”* This indicates the blow hit the front of his head (the “third eye” is on the forehead).

**Step 2:** Evidence 6 (Shichishito) states: *“DeMasque knocked Atmey out during the crime w/ a blow to the back of the head.”* This says the blow struck the back of Atmey’s head.

**Step 3:** The spatial reasoning rule *“If XXX has their back to YYY then XXX cannot see YYY”* implies that when Atmey’s back is to the wall he is facing away from the wall, so a blow to the back of his head would come from the direction of the wall. The testimony, however, says the blow hit his forehead, which is the opposite side. Thus the two statements cannot both be true about the same attack.

**Step 4:** Even without a specific rule, the contradiction is purely spatial: one source says the impact was on the back of the head, the other says it was on the front (forehead/third eye). Both cannot be correct for a single blow.

**Conclusion:** The contradiction is between **Evidence 6** and **Testimony 5**.

**Step 1:** The testimony states that the victim was “shoved the victim down from behind” (Testimony 3). The witness’s photo (Evidence 6) shows the victim standing on the bridge, still upright, facing left, with the attacker standing opposite her. **Step 3:** Applying the rule, being hit from behind would cause the victim to fall forward (i.e., toward the attacker). The photo, however, depicts the victim upright and not fallen, directly contradicting the testimony’s claim of a behind‑shove.

**Step 4:** No other listed rules are needed; the contradiction is identified by the mismatch between the expected physical outcome of a behind attack (falling) and the visual evidence of the victim remaining standing.

**Step 1 – Identify the spatial contradiction**  
Testimony 4 says L'Belle fled “down the hallway to my right – the one with the PHONE at the end.”  
Evidence 5 (Crime Scene Diagram) places the phone in a *north* dead‑end corridor, while the foyer is to the *south* and the Fox‑Chamber corridor is to the *east*. No hallway to the right of L'Belle’s position (as described) leads to the phone; the phone is straight ahead (north) from the junction, not to the right.

**Step 2 – Apply spatial reasoning rules**  
The rule *“If XXX is inside YYY and YYY is to the right of ZZZ then XXX is to the right of the ZZZ”* can be used to infer that any object inside a region that is to the right of another region is also to the right of that other region. Here, the phone (inside the north dead‑end) is **north**, not right, of the junction. Therefore the statement that the hallway “to the right … has the phone at the end” violates the spatial relationship defined by the diagram.

**Step 3 – Explain the contradiction**  
Because the diagram explicitly locates the phone north of the junction, a hallway “to the right” cannot contain the phone. L'Belle’s testimony about fleeing right toward the phone contradicts the spatial layout shown in Evidence 5.

**Step 4 – Conclusion**  
The contradictory pair is **Evidence 5** (Crime Scene Diagram) and **Testimony 4** (L'Belle’s description of fleeing right toward the phone).

**Step 1:**  
Testimony 0 (by Fairplay) states that the assailant had blood on **both** hands.  
Evidence 3 (Defendant’s Leather Gloves) notes a bloodstain **only on the right glove**.

**Step 2:**  
The spatial reasoning rules concern directions, left/right hand usage, and relative positions. The contradiction here is about the presence of blood on the left hand versus right hand, which relates to the rule:

- *If left‑hand fingerprints of HHH are on XXX then HHH held XXX with the left hand*  
- *If right‑hand fingerprints of HHH are on XXX then HHH held XXX with the right hand*

These rules imply that evidence of a bloodstain on a glove indicates which hand was involved. Since only the right glove is stained, the left hand shows no blood, contradicting the testimony that both hands were bloody.

**Step 3:**  
Applying the rule “If right‑hand fingerprints … then HHH held XXX with the right hand” tells us the right glove (and thus right hand) was bloodied. The absence of a similar statement for the left glove means the left hand was not bloodied, directly opposing the claim that **both** hands were bloodied.

**Step 4:**  
Even without a formal rule, the simple spatial fact that a glove covers a specific hand makes the presence of blood on only one glove incompatible with a claim that both hands were bloodied.

**Conclusion:** The contradiction is between **Evidence 3** and **Testimony 0**.

**Step 1:**  
Evidence 6 (Witness’s Photo) shows the victim standing on the bridge, wearing a scarf and a hat, facing left. A man in a jail uniform with chains is standing to the left of her, facing right. Thus the victim is looking directly at the man – the man is **in front of** the victim.  

Testimony 3 (by Gumshoe) states: “the criminal shoved the victim **down from behind** and stabbed her in the back!” If the attacker shoved her from behind, the victim’s back would be to the attacker and she would **not** be facing him.

**Step 2:**  
The spatial rule “If XXX is facing YYY then XXX can see YYY” applies.

**Step 3:**  
Applying the rule: because the victim is facing the man (as shown in Evidence 6), the victim can see the man, meaning the man is in front of her, not behind her. This directly contradicts Testimony 3, which claims the attacker was behind the victim.

**Step 4:**  
No other listed rules are needed; the contradiction is identified by the spatial relationship of “facing” versus “behind” as depicted in the photo versus described in the testimony.
